Proposition 8 (Minimal pair inductive structure).
Let $(G,f)$ be a minimal graph-nonedge pair with a top-level expanded $f$ -separating CMS $C^{-1}$ whose expanded $f$ -component is $H^{-1}$ , and let $G^{\prime}=G\setminus(H^{-1}\setminus C^{-1})$ .
There exists a nonedge $f^{\prime}=uv$ of $C^{-1}$ and an induced subgraph $J$ of $G^{\prime}$ that contains $f^{\prime}$ such that $(J,f^{\prime})$ is minimal and $G^{\prime}$ can be obtained from $J$ by adding some vertices whose neighborhoods are the set $\{u,v\}$ .
Furthermore, these properties are true when (i) $f^{\prime}=w_{1}w_{3}$ and $H^{-1}$ is the graph in Figure 20(c), $f$ is the red edge, and $C^{-1}$ is the subgraph induced by $\{w_{i}\}$ , or (ii) $f^{\prime}$ is any nonedge of $C^{-1}$ and $H^{-1}$ is the graph in Figure 20(d), $f$ is the red edge, and $C^{-1}$ is the subgraph induced by $\{w_{i}\}$ .
Proposition 9 (Pruned possible graphs for top-level $H^{-1}$ ).
Let $H^{-1}$ be the expanded $f$ -component of any top-level expanded $f$ -separating CMS $C^{-1}$ of a minimal graph-nonedge pair.
Then, $H^{-1}$ is one of the graphs in Figures 20(a) - 20(e) such that the dashed line-segment is a nonedge, $f$ is the red edge, and $C^{-1}$ is the subgraph induced by $\{w_{i}\}$ .
The proofs of Propositions 8 and 9 are very technical and involve extensive case analyses, and so we devote a separate Section 5.1 to them.
Proposition 10 is proved using Lemma 24, below.
 [FIGURE Refer to caption] 
(a) 
 [FIGURE Refer to caption] 
(b) 
Figure 21: Realizations in the proof of Lemma 24. 
Lemma 24 (Degree $3$ decorations preserve non- $3$ -SIP).
Let $G$ be $K_{4}$ and $H$ be one of its $K_{3}$ subgraphs.
Let $\ell_{1}$ and $\ell_{2}$ be any squared edge-length maps for $H$ such that some edge is assigned distinct positive real numbers by both maps.
If the linkages $(H,\ell_{1})$ and $(H,\ell_{2})$ both have $2$ -realizations, then there exist squared edge-length maps $\ell^{\prime}_{1}$ and $\ell^{\prime}_{2}$ for $G$ such that
(i) 
they agree with each other on the edges not in $H$ and assign these edges positive real numbers,
(ii) 
 $\ell^{\prime}_{i}$ agrees with $\ell_{i}$ on the edges of $H$ ,
(iii) 
the linkages $(G,\ell^{\prime}_{1})$ and $(G,\ell^{\prime}_{2})$ both have $2$ -realizations.
Lemma 24 is proved in B.4.
The crux of the proof is illustrated in Figure 21: consider realizations $p$ and $q$ of $(H,\ell_{1})$ and $(H,\ell_{2})$ , respectively, with $p_{1}$ and $q_{1}$ at the origin and with $p_{2}$ and $q_{2}$ on the positive $x$ -axis.
All points equidistant from $p_{2}$ and $q_{2}$ lie on the dashed blue line, and all points equidistant from $p_{3}$ and $q_{3}$ lie on the dashed red line.
We show that the $G$ linkages can be chosen so that the realization $p$ is rotated to a position where these lines intersect, and then the lemma easily follows.
Proposition 10 (Extending non- $3$ -SIP maps for minimal pairs).
Let $G^{\prime}$ be any of the graphs in Figures 20(a) - 20(e) such that the dashed line-segment is a nonedge, let $H$ be the subgraph of $G$ induced by the set $\{w_{i}\}$ , let $f$ be the red edge, and let $f^{\prime}$ be the nonedge of $H$ that does not share an endpoint with $f$ if possible, or any other nonedge of $H$ otherwise.
Also, let $G=(G^{\prime}\setminus f)\cup f^{\prime}$ .
Lastly, let $\ell_{1}$ and $\ell_{2}$ be squared edge-length maps for $H$ that assign each edge a positive real number and differ only in the numbers assigned to $f^{\prime}$ .
If the linkages $(H,\ell_{1})$ and $(H,\ell_{2})$ both have at least one $3$ -realization, then there exist squared edge-length maps $\ell^{\prime}_{1}$ and $\ell^{\prime}_{2}$ for $G$ such that
(i) 
they agree with each other on the edges not in $H$ and assign these edges positive real numbers,
(ii) 
 $\ell^{\prime}_{i}$ agrees with $\ell_{i}$ on the edges of $H$ , and
(iii) 
the CCSs $\Omega^{3}_{f}(G,\ell^{\prime}_{1})$ and $\Omega^{3}_{f}(G,\ell^{\prime}_{2})$ each contain exactly one positive value, and these values are distinct.
Proof.
If $G^{\prime}$ is one of the graphs in Figures 20(a) - 20(c), then Lemma 18 applies and proves the proposition.
Else, if $G^{\prime}$ is the graph in Figure 20(d), then we can first apply Lemma 18 to the subgraph of $G^{\prime}$ obtained by deleting the unlabeled vertices, and then add these vertices back and apply Lemma 24 to prove the proposition.
Lastly, if $G^{\prime}$ is the graph in Figure 20(e), then consider the subgraph of $G^{\prime}$ obtained by deleting all but one unlabeled vertex, which we call $x$ .
We first apply Lemma 18 to this subgraph while treating $xw_{2}$ as $f$ , and then we apply this lemma again while treating $xw_{2}$ as $f^{\prime}$ and $uw_{1}$ as $f$ .
Finally, we add the deleted vertices and apply Lemma 24 to prove the proposition.
∎
We are now ready to prove Theorem 7 and the forward direction of Theorem 4.
Proof of Theorem 7.
We show that a minimal graph-nonedge pair $(G,f)$ has a proper non- $3$ -SIP map by induction on the number $n$ of vertices in $G$ .
Since $G\cup f$ has a forbidden minor, we have $n\geq 5$ .
When $n=5$ , $G\cup f$ is $K_{5}$ , and so the theorem follows from Proposition 6.
Assume that the theorem is true when $n\leq k$ , for any integer $k\geq 5$ , and we will show that it is true when $n=k+1$ .
The same proposition allows us to assume that $(G,f)$ has a Type (2) edge.
Hence, $(G,f)$ has a top-level expanded $f$ -separating CMS $C^{-1}$ , by Proposition 7.
Let $H^{-1}$ be the expanded $f$ -component of $C^{-1}$ and $G^{\prime}=G\setminus(H^{-1}\setminus C^{-1})$ .
Consider any minimal graph-nonedge pair $(J,f^{\prime})$ obtained by applying Proposition 8 to $(G,f)$ and $C^{-1}$ .
Proposition 9 tells us that $H^{-1}$ is one of the graphs in Figures 20(a) - 20(e) such that the dashed line-segment is a nonedge, $f$ is the red edge, and $C^{-1}$ is the subgraph induced by $\{w_{i}\}$ .
Proposition 8 allows us to choose $f^{\prime}=w_{1}w_{3}$ if $H^{-1}$ is the graph in Figure 20(c), and $f^{\prime}=w_{2}w_{4}$ if $H^{-1}$ is the graph in Figure 20(d).
Next, since $J$ has strictly fewer vertices than $G$ , the inductive hypothesis applies and states that $(J,f^{\prime})$ has a proper non- $3$ -SIP map.
Using the relationship between $G^{\prime}$ and $J$ described in Proposition 8 along with the triangle-inequality, we can obtain a proper non- $3$ -SIP map $\ell_{G^{\prime}}$ for $(G^{\prime},f^{\prime})$ .
By definition, this means that the CCS $\Omega^{3}_{f^{\prime}}(G^{\prime},\ell_{G^{\prime}})$ contains exactly two distinct positive real numbers.
Let $\ell_{1}$ and $\ell_{2}$ be squared edge-length maps for the edges of $C^{-1}\cup f^{\prime}$ that agree with $\ell_{G^{\prime}}$ on the edges of $C^{-1}$ and assign different squared lengths from $\Omega^{3}_{f^{\prime}}(G^{\prime},\ell_{G^{\prime}})$ to $f^{\prime}$ .
Note that the linkages $(C^{-1}\cup f^{\prime},\ell_{1})$ and $(C^{-1}\cup f^{\prime},\ell_{2})$ each have at least one $3$ -realization.
Therefore, consider the edge-length maps $\ell^{\prime}_{1}$ and $\ell^{\prime}_{2}$ for the edges of $(H^{-1}\setminus f)\cup f^{\prime}$ given by Proposition 10.
Define $\ell_{G}$ to be the edge-length map for the edges of $G$ that agrees with $\ell_{G^{\prime}}$ on the edges of $G^{\prime}$ and $\ell^{\prime}_{1}$ on the edges of $H^{-1}$ .
Clearly, $\ell_{G}$ is a proper non- $3$ -SIP map for $(G,f)$ , which completes the proof by induction.
∎
Proof of the forward direction of Theorem 4.
Let $(G,f)$ be a graph-nonedge pair such that some atom of $G\cup f$ that contains $f$ has an $f$ -preserving forbidden minor.
Corollary 3 allows us to assume wlog that $G\cup f$ is an atom.
If $(G,f)$ has a reducing edge, then let $[G\cup f]$ be the minor obtained by contracting any such edge.
By definition, $[G\cup f]$ is $f$ -retaining has an atom that contains $[f]$ and an $[f]$ -preserving forbidden minor.
Hence, by Lemma 1, if $([G\cup f]\setminus[f],[f])$ does not have the $3$ -SIP, then neither does $(G,f)$ .
Therefore, we can assume wlog that $(G,f)$ has no reducing edge.
Combining this with the fact that $G\cup f$ is an atom shows that $(G,f)$ is minimal, and thus the theorem follows from Theorem 7.
∎
5.1 Proofs of Propositions 8 and 9
Consider the graph-nonedge pair $(G_{\star},f_{\star})$ from Section 5, let $C^{-1}$ be any top-level expanded $f_{\star}$ -separating CMS of $G_{\star}\cup f_{\star}$ , and let $H^{-1}$ be its expanded $f_{\star}$ -component.
We present the highly technical proofs of Proposition 8, which demonstrates the above-mentioned nested structure of minimal pairs, and Proposition 9, which lists all possible graphs for $H^{-1}$ .
The proofs are completed in two parts: the first part enumerates a finite set of candidate graphs for $H^{-1}$ and the second part gradually eliminates all but a few of these candidates.
The first part begins with Lemma 25, below, which enumerates all possible graphs for $C^{-1}$ and describes the resulting structure on $G_{\star}\cup f_{\star}$ .
Recall from Section 3 that an induced subgraph $H$ of a graph $G$ is retained in a minor $[G]$ if edge and nonedge of $H$ is retained in $[G]$ .
For any induced subgraphs $H_{1}$ and $H_{2}$ of $G$ , $H_{1}\cup H_{2}$ is the subgraph of $G$ induced by $V(H_{1})\cup V(H_{2})$ .

See the discussion and Lemmas 25 and 30 in this section.

Figure 23: Graphs in Lemma 25.

Lemma 25 (Unpruned possible graphs for top-level $C^{-1}$ ).
Let $C^{-1}$ be any top-level expanded $f$ -separating CMS of a minimal graph-nonedge pair $(G,f)$ , and let its expanded $f$ -component and expanded minor component be $H^{-1}$ and $I^{-1}$ , respectively.
Then, the following statements are true:
1. 
 $C^{-1}$ is
one of the graphs in Figure 22 and
2. 
the minor $[H^{-1}\cup I^{-1}]$ obtained by contracting every edge in both $H^{-1}\setminus C^{-1}$ and $I^{-1}\setminus C^{-1}$ is one of the graphs in Figure 23 with $[C^{-1}]$ as the subgraph induced by the set $\{w_{i}\}$ .
The proof of Lemma 25 is given in B.2.
The main obstacle to proving Statement (1) is the case where $C^{-1}$ has at least six vertices, which we show is not possible when $C^{-1}$ is in the top-level.
Statement (2) then follows from the facts that $G_{\star}\cup f_{\star}$ is an atom and $C^{-1}$ is an expanded $f$ -separating CMS.
Next, Lemma 28, below, describes the neighborhoods of vertices in $H^{-1}\setminus C^{-1}$ , showing that each edge of this subgraph is incident to some endpoint of $f_{\star}$ .
This lemma is proved using using Lemmas 26 and 27, also below.
Lemma 26 (No Type (1) edge in $(H^{-1}\setminus f)\setminus C^{-1}$ ).
Let $C^{-1}$ be any expanded $f$ -separating CMS of a minimal graph-nonedge pair $(G,f)$ , with $f=uv$ , and let its expanded $f$ -component and expanded minor component be $H^{-1}$ and $I^{-1}$ , respectively.
Contracting any edge of $(H^{-1}\setminus f)\setminus C^{-1}$ yields a minor $[G\cup f]$ such that $[I^{-1}]$ has an $[f]$ -preserving forbidden minor.
Lemma 27 (No Type (2) edge in $(H^{-1}\setminus f)\setminus C^{-1}$ ).
Let $C^{-1}$ be an expanded $f$ -separating CMS of a minimal graph-nonedge pair $(G,f)$ , and let $H^{-1}$ be its expanded $f$ -component.
If $C^{-1}$ is in the top level of $(G,f)$ , then no edge of $(H^{-1}\setminus f)\setminus C^{-1}$ is of Type (2).
Lemmas 26 and 27 are proved in B.2.
To prove Lemma 26, we show that the $f_{\star}$ -preserving forbidden minor of $I^{-1}$ , which exists by definition, is not destroyed by the contraction leading to $[G_{\star}\cup f_{\star}]$ .
Lemma 27 is proved by assuming to the contrary that $(H^{-1}\setminus f_{\star})\setminus C^{-1}$ contains some Type (2) edge and then concluding that $C^{-1}$ is not in the top-level, a contradiction.
Lemma 28 (Structure of top-level $H^{-1}$ ).
Let $C^{-1}$ be a top-level expanded $f$ -separating CMS of a minimal graph-nonedge pair $(G,f)$ , with $f=uv$ , and let $H^{-1}$ be its expanded $f$ -component.
The neighborhood of any vertex $w$ in $H^{-1}\setminus(C^{-1}\cup\{u,v\})$ ) contains only $u$ , $v$ , and at least one vertex in $C^{-1}\setminus\{u,v\}$ .
Furthermore, the vertices $u$ and $v$ may have neighbors in $C^{-1}$ .
Proof.
Since $w$ is a vertex of $H^{-1}\setminus(C^{-1}\cup\{u,v\})$ , it must be adjacent to some vertex $z$ in $H^{-1}\setminus C^{-1}$ .
Note that Lemmas 26 and 27 imply that $wz$ is of Type (3).
Hence, $z$ must be either $u$ or $v$ , and both $wu$ and $wv$ must be edges.
Next, since $G\cup f$ is an atom, $w$ must be adjacent to some vertex other than $u$ or $v$ .
The same two lemmas imply that this vertex is contained in $C^{-1}$ .
Thus, the lemma is proved.
∎
Lemmas 25 and 28 are used to prove Lemma 30, below, which eliminates the graphs in Figures 22(e)-(i) as possibilities for $C^{-1}$ .
The proof of Lemma 30 also requires Lemma 29, below.

Lemma 29 (Cases for $H^{-1}$ when $C^{-1}$ has $5$ vertices).
Let $C^{-1}$ be a top-level expanded $f$ -separating CMS of a minimal graph-nonedge pair $(G,f)$ .
If $|V(C^{-1})|=5$ , then the expanded $f$ -component of $C^{-1}$ is one of the graphs in Figure 24 with $f$ as the red edge and $C^{-1}$ as the subgraph induced by the set $\{w_{i}\}$ .
Lemma 30 (Pruned possible graphs for $C^{-1}$ ).
If $C^{-1}$ is a top-level expanded $f$ -separating CMS of a minimal graph-nonedge pair $(G,f)$ , then $C^{-1}$ is one of the graphs in Figures 22(a)-22(d).
Lemmas 29 and 30 are proved in Section B.2.
Lemma 29 is proved by showing that, in any $f_{\star}$ -separating minor $[G_{\star}\cup f_{\star}]$ where $[C^{-1}]$ is a CMS with four vertices, $[H^{-1}]$ is the graph in Figure 8(a) such that $[f_{\star}]$ is $w_{1}w_{2}$ .
The lemma then follows easily from Lemma 25 Statement (2).
Lemma 30 is proved by assuming to the contrary that $C^{-1}$ is one of the graphs in Figures 22(e)-(i) and then concluding that $(G_{\star},f_{\star})$ is not minimal, a contradiction.
The above lemmas have considerably reduced the possible graphs for $H^{-1}$ , which are now enumerated in Lemma 31, below.
The proof of this lemma, given in B.2.1, involves case checking using techniques as in the proof of Lemma 30, but for far more cases.
Lemma 31 (Unpruned possible graphs for top-level $H^{-1}$ ).
If $C^{-1}$ is a top-level expanded $f$ -separating CMS of a minimal graph-nonedge pair $(G,f)$ , then the expanded $f$ -component of $C^{-1}$ is one of the graphs in Figure 20 with $f$ as the red edge and $C^{-1}$ as the subgraph induced by the vertex set $\{w_{i}\}$ .
Next, Lemma 32, stated below and proved in B.2, reduces the possible graphs for $H^{-1}$ to those in Figures 20(a) - 20(m).
The proof argues that $(G_{\star},f_{\star})$ has a reducing edge if $H^{-1}$ is not one of these graphs, a contradiction.
Lemma 32 (Pruning cases for $H^{-1}$ ).
The expanded $f$ -component $H^{-1}$ of any expanded $f$ -separating CMS $C^{-1}$ of a minimal graph-nonedge pair is one of the graphs in Figures 20(a) - 20(m), where $C^{-1}$ is the subgraph induced by the vertex set $\{w_{i}\}$ .
Furthermore, if $H^{-1}$ is one of the graphs in Figures 20(c), 20(d), 20(e), 20(i), 20(k), or 20(l), then at least one dashed line-segment is a nonedge.
All this reduction work leads to Lemmas 33 and 34, below, from which Proposition 8 immediately follows.
The proof of Lemma 33, given in B.2.2, is another instance of checking many cases.
Lemma 33 (Additional structure necessary for induction).
Let $(G,f)$ be a minimal graph-nonedge pair with a top-level expanded $f$ -separating CMS $C^{-1}$ whose expanded $f$ -component is $H^{-1}$ , and let $G^{\prime}=G\setminus(H^{-1}\setminus C^{-1})$ .
There exists a nonedge $f^{\prime}$ of $C^{-1}$ such that some atom of $G^{\prime}\cup f^{\prime}$ contains $f^{\prime}$ and has an $f^{\prime}$ -preserving forbidden minor and the pair $(G^{\prime},f^{\prime})$ has no reducing edge.
Furthermore, these statements are true when (i) $f^{\prime}=w_{1}w_{3}$ and $H^{-1}$ is the graph in Figure 20(c), $f$ is the red edge, and $C^{-1}$ is the subgraph induced by $\{w_{i}\}$ , or (ii) $f^{\prime}$ is any nonedge of $C^{-1}$ and $H^{-1}$ is one of the graphs in Figures 20(d), 20(i), or 20(j), $f$ is the red edge, and $C^{-1}$ is the subgraph induced by $\{w_{i}\}$ .
Lemma 34 (Structure of $G^{\prime}\cup f^{\prime}$ ).
Consider a minimal graph-nonedge pair $(G,f)$ with a top-level expanded $f$ -separating CMS $C^{-1}$ whose expanded $f$ -component is $H^{-1}$ , and let $G^{\prime}=G\setminus(H^{-1}\setminus C^{-1})$ .
If $f^{\prime}=uv$ is a nonedge of $C^{-1}$ such that some atom $J$ of $G^{\prime}\cup f^{\prime}$ contains $f^{\prime}$ and has an $f^{\prime}$ -preserving forbidden minor and the pair $(G^{\prime},f^{\prime})$ has no reducing edge, then $G^{\prime}\cup f^{\prime}$ can be obtained from $J$ by adding some vertices whose neighborhoods are $\{u,v\}$ .
Proof.
Assume to the contrary that $G^{\prime}\cup f^{\prime}$ cannot be obtained from $J$ by adding vertices whose neighborhood are $\{u,v\}$ .
Then, there exists a CMS $E$ of $G^{\prime}\cup f^{\prime}$ contained in $J$ such that $E$ contains some vertex $x$ that is either the only vertex in $E$ or some vertex other than $u$ or $v$ .
Also, there exists a vertex $y$ not contained in $J$ and such that $xy$ is an edge of $G^{\prime}\cup f^{\prime}$ and all paths in $G^{\prime}\cup f^{\prime}$ between $y$ and any vertex in $J\setminus E$ contains some vertex in $E$ .
Hence, $J$ is weakly retained in the minor $[G^{\prime}\cup f^{\prime}]$ obtained by contracting $xy$ .
Furthermore, $[G^{\prime}\cup f^{\prime}]$ is $f^{\prime}$ -retaining and contains some atom that contains $[f^{\prime}]$ and has an $[f^{\prime}]$ -preserving forbidden minor.
The above facts show that $xy$ is reducing with respect to $(G^{\prime},f^{\prime})$ , which is a contradiction.
∎
Proof of Proposition 8.
The proposition follows easily from Lemmas 33 and 34.
∎
Finally, Proposition 9 follows immediately from Lemmas 35 and 36, stated below and proved in B.3, which are the final reductions of the possible graphs for $H^{-1}$ .
The proofs of both lemmas argue that $(G_{\star},f_{\star})$ has a reducing edge if either lemma is false, a contradiction.
Lemma 35 (Additional pruning of cases for $H^{-1}$ ).
For any minimal graph-nonedge pair $(G,f)$ with a top-level expanded $f$ -separating CMS $C^{-1}$ , the expanded $f$ -component of $C^{-1}$ is one of the graphs in Figures 20(a) - 20(h), where $C^{-1}$ is the subgraph induced by the vertex set $\{w_{i}\}$ .
Furthermore, if $H^{-1}$ is one of the graphs in Figures 20(c), 20(d), or 20(e), then the dashed line-segment is a nonedge.
Lemma 36 ( $H^{-1}$ is not graph in Figures 20(f)-20(h)).
None of the following graphs are the expanded $f$ -component of any top-level expanded $f$ -separating CMS of a minimal graph-nonedge pair:
(i) 
the graph in Figure 20(f),
(ii) 
neither of the graphs in Figures 20(g) or 20(h) with $uw_{1}$ being an edge, or
(iii) 
the graph in Figure 20(h) with $uw_{1}$ being a nonedge.
Proof of Proposition 9.
The proposition follows immediately from Lemmas 35 and 36.
∎
6 Open problems and conjectures
Open Problem 1.
Give a polynomial time algorithm to decide the characterization in Theorem 4.
The polynomial-time algorithm in [19] for finite forbidden minors will likely be useful for this problem.
Conjecture 2.
For any dimension $d\geq 1$ , a graph-nonedge pair $(G,f)$ has the $d$ -SIP if and only if no atom of $G\cup f$ that contains $f$ has an $f$ -preserving $d$ -forbidden minor.
Since the number of forbidden minors for $d$ -flattenability is conjectured to grow asymptotically with the number of forbidden minors for partial $d$ -trees [17], which is at least $75$ for $d=4$ [36], a proof of this conjecture must avoid using properties of the explicit $d$ -flattenability forbidden minors.
To this end, we posit the following conjecture.
Conjecture 3.
For any dimension $d\geq 2$ , a graph $G$ is a forbidden minor for $d$ -flattenability if and only if both of the following are true:
1. 
for any edge $e$ of $G$ , $(G\setminus e,e)$ does not have the $d$ -SIP and
2. 
for any minor $[G]$ obtained via a single edge deletion or contraction and any edge edge $e$ of $[G]$ , $([G]\setminus e,e)$ has the $d$ -SIP.
Finally, an important motivation for studying the $d$ -SIP is to characterize graphs $G$ and nonedge sets $F$ for which $(G,F)$ is $d$ -convex.
Recall Theorem 1 states that, for any dimension $d\leq 2$ , $(G,F)$ is $d$ -convex if and only if each atom of $G\cup F$ that contains a non-empty subset of $F$ has no $K_{d+2}$ minor.
The following is an equivalent restatement of the right-hand-side of this theorem via Theorem 2: for any $f\in F$ , $(G\cup(F\setminus\{f\},f)$ has the $d$ -SIP.
Conjecture 4.
For any dimension $d\geq 1$ and any graph $G$ with nonedge set $F$ , $(G,F)$ is $d$ -convex if and only if, for any $f\in F$ , $(G\cup(F\setminus\{f\},f)$ has the $d$ -SIP.

Appendix A Proofs for the converse direction
A.1 Proof of Lemma 9 for Proposition 3
Proof of Lemma 9.
The proof is split into three cases: some set in $\{X,Y\}$ has size one, some set in $\{A,B\}$ has size one, and each set in $\{X,Y,A,B\}$ has size at least two.
We prove the lemma in the first case.
The other two cases are similar and involve inspecting many cases, so we omit them, but they are available upon request.
We further focus on the case where $[G\cup f]$ is the graph in Figure 8(a).
The case where $[G\cup f]$ is the graph in Figure 8(b) is similar, so we omit the details but highlight the differences at the end of the proof.
Wlog, assume that $|Y|=1$ and $|X|>1$ .
We will show how to perform a sequence of vertex and component exchanges that transform $[G\cup f]$ into the desired $f$ -winged graph minor $[G\cup f]_{1}$ .
Let $H$ be the subgraph of $G\cup f$ induced by $[c_{3}]^{-1}$ and consider any vertex $a\in A$ with a neighbor in $[c_{5}]^{-1}$ .
Since $|X|>1$ , some connected component $H_{1}$ of $H\setminus a$ contains a vertex in $X$ .
There are four cases.
Case 1: $H_{1}$ contains some vertices with neighbors in $[c_{4}]^{-1}$ and $[c_{6}]^{-1}$ .
Let $[G\cup f]_{1}$ be obtained via the vertex exchange from $[c_{3}]^{-1}$ to $[c_{5}]^{-1}$ that exchanges $a$ and fixes $H_{1}$ .
Clearly, $[G\cup f]_{1}$ is one of the graphs in Figures 15(a), 15(b), or 8(a) such that $[f]_{1}$ is $w_{1}w_{2}$ , $[d_{3}]_{1}^{-1}\cup[d_{4}]_{1}^{-1}$ is a proper subset of $[c_{3}]^{-1}\cup[c_{4}]^{-1}$ , and both $[f]_{1}^{-1}$ and $[f]^{-1}$ contain the same edges of $G$ .
Using Lemma 5, we see that either $G\cup f$ has an $f$ -preserving forbidden minor, which is a contradiction, or $[G\cup f]_{1}$ has the desired properties.
Note 4.
In the remainder of the proof, we examine many cases for which similar arguments apply.
As to not overburden the proof, we will state how to obtain $[G\cup f]_{1}$ and make observations about neighborhoods of vertices, and we leave it to the reader to use these observations along with Lemma 5 to verify that either $G\cup f$ has an $f$ -preserving forbidden minor, which is a contradiction, or $[G\cup f]_{1}$ has the desired properties.
Case 2: $H_{1}$ does not contain a vertex with a neighbor in either $[c_{4}]^{-1}$ or $[c_{6}]^{-1}$ .
Note that $H\setminus H_{1}$ contains some vertices with neighbors in $[c_{4}]^{-1}$ and $[c_{6}]^{-1}$ .
Let $[G\cup f]_{1}$ be the minor obtained via the component exchange from $[c_{3}]^{-1}$ to $[c_{1}]^{-1}$ that exchanges $H_{1}$ .
Case 3: $H_{1}$ contains some vertex with a neighbor in $[c_{6}]^{-1}$ but no vertex with a neighbor in $[c_{4}]^{-1}$ .
Let $a^{\prime}$ be any vertex in $H_{1}$ with a neighbor in $[c_{6}]^{-1}$ and note that $H\setminus H_{1}$ is a subgraph of some connected component $J_{1}$ of $H\setminus a^{\prime}$ and contains some vertex with a neighbor in $[c_{4}]^{-1}$ .
If some connected component $J_{2}$ of $H\setminus a^{\prime}$ other than $J_{1}$ contains some vertex in $X$ , then let $[G\cup f]_{1}$ be the minor obtained via the component exchange from $[c_{3}]^{-1}$ to $[c_{1}]^{-1}$ that exchanges $J_{2}$ .
Otherwise, $J_{1}$ contains some vertex in $X$ since $|X|\geq 2$ .
Hence, let $[G\cup f]_{1}$ be the minor obtained via the vertex exchange from $[c_{3}]^{-1}$ to $[c_{6}]^{-1}$ that exchanges $a^{\prime}$ and fixes $J_{1}$ .
Case 4: $H_{1}$ contains some vertex with a neighbor in $[c_{4}]^{-1}$ but no vertex with a neighbor in $[c_{6}]^{-1}$ .
This case contains many subcases.
The reader should keep in mind the edges and nonedges of $[G\cup f]$ as well as the assumptions leading to each subcase.
Note that $H\setminus H_{1}$ contains some vertex with a neighbor in $[c_{6}]^{-1}$ .
If $H\setminus H_{1}$ contains some vertex with a neighbor in $[c_{4}]^{-1}$ , then let $[G\cup f]_{1}$ be the minor obtained via the component exchange from $[c_{3}]^{-1}$ to $[c_{1}]^{-1}$ that exchanges $H_{1}$ .
Otherwise,
if $H_{1}$ contains some vertex with a neighbor in $[c_{5}]^{-1}$ , then let $[G\cup f]_{1}$ be the minor obtained via the vertex exchange from $[c_{3}]^{-1}$ to $[c_{6}]^{-1}$ that exchanges $a$ and fixes $H_{1}$ .
Hence, assume that $A\subseteq V(H-H_{1})$ .
If some connected component $H_{2}$ of $H\setminus a$ other than $H_{1}$ contains some vertex with a neighbor in $[c_{6}]^{-1}$ , then let $[G\cup f]_{1}$ be the minor obtained via the component exchange from $[c_{3}]^{-1}$ to $[c_{6}]^{-1}$ that exchanges $H_{2}$ .
Otherwise, $a$ has some neighbor in $[c_{6}]^{-1}$ .
If $H_{2}$ contains some vertex with a neighbor in $[c_{5}]^{-1}$ , then $[G\cup f]_{1}$ is the minor obtained via the component exchange from $[c_{3}]^{-1}$ to $[c_{5}]^{-1}$ that exchanges $H_{2}$ .
Therefore, assume that $A=\{a\}$ , and so $|B|\geq 2$ by assumption.
Hence, there exists a vertex $b\in B\setminus Y$ since $|Y|=1$ .
Wlog, assume that $b$ neighbors some vertex in $[c_{5}]^{-1}$ .
Let $K$ be the subgraph of $G\cup f$ induced by $[c_{4}]^{-1}$ and let $K_{1}$ be the connected component of $K\setminus b$ that contains the vertex $y\in Y$ .
By arguments similar to those above, we can conclude that either (a) $K_{1}$ contains some vertex with a neighbor in $[c_{3}]^{-1}$ but no vertex with a neighbor in $[c_{6}]^{-1}$ or (b) $K_{1}$ contains some vertex with a neighbor in $[c_{6}]^{-1}$ but no vertex with a neighbor in $[c_{3}]^{-1}$ .
Assume that Statement (a) is true, and so $K\setminus K_{1}$ contains some vertex with a neighbor in $[c_{6}]^{-1}$ .
If $K_{1}$ contains some vertex with a neighbor in $[c_{5}]^{-1}$ , then let $[G\cup f]_{1}$ be the minor obtained via the vertex exchange from $[c_{4}]^{-1}$ to $[c_{6}]^{-1}$ that exchanges $b$ and fixes $K_{1}$ .
Otherwise, we have that $B\subseteq V(K\setminus K_{1})$ .
Hence, some connected component $K_{2}$ of $K\setminus b$ other than $K_{1}$ contains some vertex in $B$ .
If $K_{2}$ contains some vertex with a neighbor in $[c_{6}]^{-1}$ , then let $[G\cup f]_{1}$ be the minor obtained via the component exchange from $[c_{4}]^{-1}$ to $[c_{6}]^{-1}$ that exchanges $K_{2}$ .
Otherwise, $b$ must have some neighbor in $[c_{6}]^{-1}$ .
In this case, let $[G\cup f]_{1}$ be the minor obtained via the component exchange from $[c_{4}]^{-1}$ to $[c_{5}]^{-1}$ that exchanges $K_{2}$ .
Next, assume that Statement (b) is true, and so $K\setminus K_{1}$ contains some vertex with a neighbor $w\in[c_{3}]^{-1}$ .
By a previous assumption, we have $w\in V(H_{1})$ .
Hence, $H\setminus H_{1}$ is a subgraph of some connected component $J_{1}$ of $H\setminus w$ .
If some connected component $J_{2}$ of $H\setminus w$ other than $J_{1}$ contains some vertex in $X$ , then let $[G\cup f]_{1}$ be the minor obtained via the component exchange from $[c_{3}]^{-1}$ to $[c_{1}]^{-1}$ that exchanges $J_{2}$ .
Otherwise, $J_{1}$ contains some vertex in $X$ since $|X|\geq 2$ .
If $w\in X$ , then consider the minor $[G\cup f]_{2}$ obtained via the component exchange from $[c_{4}]^{-1}$ to $[c_{6}]^{-1}$ that exchanges $K_{1}$ , and let it have have the vertex set $\{[b_{i}]_{2}\}$ .
Let $[G\cup f]_{1}$ be the minor obtained via the vertex exchange from $[b_{3}]_{2}^{-1}$ to $[b_{4}]_{2}^{-1}$ that exchanges $w$ and fixes $J_{1}$ .
Otherwise,
if some connected component $H_{2}$ of $H\setminus a$ other than $H_{1}$ contains some vertex in $X$ , then let $[G\cup f]_{1}$ be the minor obtained via the component exchange from $[c_{3}]^{-1}$ to $[c_{1}]^{-1}$ that exchanges $H_{2}$ .
Therefore, it must be that $J_{1}\cap H_{1}$ contains some vertex in $X$ .
If $a\in X$ , then consider the minor $[G\cup f]_{2}$ obtained via the component exchange from $[c_{4}]^{-1}$ to $[c_{6}]^{-1}$ that exchanges $K_{1}$ , and let it have the vertex set $\{[b_{i}]_{2}\}$ .
Let $[G\cup f]_{1}$ be the minor obtained via the component exchange from $[b_{3}]_{2}^{-1}$ to $[b_{4}]_{2}^{-1}$ that exchanges $H_{1}$ .
Lastly, by the above arguments, it suffices to assume that $X\subseteq V(J_{1}\cap H_{1})$ .
Consider any vertex $x_{1}\in X$ and let $R_{1}$ be the connected component of $H\setminus x_{1}$ that contains $w$ .
If $R_{1}$ contains $a$ , then let $[G\cup f]_{1}$ be the minor obtained via the vertex exchange from $[c_{3}]^{-1}$ to $[c_{1}]^{-1}$ that exchanges $x_{1}$ and fixes $R_{1}$ .
Otherwise, let $R_{2}$ be the connected component of $H\setminus x_{1}$ that contains $a$ .
Since $|X|\geq 2$ , there exists a vertex $x_{2}\in X$ in some connected component of $H\setminus x_{1}$ .
By the above arguments, this connected component must be either $R_{1}$ or $R_{2}$ .
If $x_{2}$ is contained in $R_{1}$ , then consider the minor $[G\cup f]_{2}$ obtained via the component exchange from $[c_{4}]^{-1}$ to $[c_{6}]^{-1}$ that exchanges $K_{1}$ , and let it have the vertex set $\{[b_{i}]_{2}\}$ .
Let $[G\cup f]_{1}$ be the minor obtained via the component exchange from $[b_{3}]_{2}^{-1}$ to $[b_{4}]_{2}^{-1}$ that exchanges $R_{1}$ .
Otherwise, let $[G\cup f]_{2}$ be obtained via the component exchange from $[c_{4}]^{-1}$ to $[c_{6}]^{-1}$ that exchanges $K_{1}$ .
Let $[G\cup f]_{1}$ be the minor obtained via the vertex exchange from $[b_{3}]_{2}^{-1}$ to $[b_{4}]_{2}^{-1}$ that exchanges $x_{1}$ and fixes $R_{2}$ .
The cases above are exhaustive, and so the lemma is proved for the case where $[G\cup f]$ is the graph in Figure 8(a).
Finally, consider the case where $[G\cup f]$ is the graph in Figure 8(b).
As stated at the beginning of this proof, the argument is similar, so we omit the details but note the differences in the argument by examining the case where $H_{1}$ some contains vertices with neighbors in $[c_{4}]^{-1}$ and $[c_{7}]^{-1}$ .
Let $[G\cup f]_{1}$ be obtained via the vertex exchange from $[c_{3}]^{-1}$ to $[c_{5}]^{-1}$ that exchanges $a$ and fixes $H_{1}$ .
Clearly, $[G\cup f]_{1}$ is some graph $G^{\prime}$ that can be obtained from one of the graphs in Figures 15(c) - 15(e) or 8(b) by adding edges between its vertices, $[f]_{1}$ is $w_{1}w_{2}$ , $[d_{3}]_{1}^{-1}\cup[d_{4}]_{1}^{-1}$ is a proper subset of $[c_{3}]^{-1}\cup[c_{4}]^{-1}$ , and both $[f]_{1}^{-1}$ and $[f]^{-1}$ contain the same edges of $G$ .
If $G^{\prime}$ is the graph Figure 8(b), then $[G\cup f]_{1}$ has the desired properties.
Otherwise, it is easy to see that $G\cup f$ has an $f$ -preserving forbidden minor, which is a contradiction.
This completes the proof.
∎
A.2 Proof of Lemma 15 for Theorem 5
Lemmas 15 follows immediately from Lemmas 37 and 38, below.
Lemma 37 (If $G\cup\{wx,wy\}$ has a forbidden minor, then so does $G\cup wx$ ).
Let $G$ be a $3$ -connected graph and $F=\{wx,wy\}$ be a set of its nonedges.
If there exists a forbidden minor $[G\cup F]$ in which $[w]$ , $[x]$ , and $[y]$ are distinct vertices and $wx$ and $wy$ are retained, then some $f\in F$ is such that $G\cup f$ has a forbidden minor.
Proof.
Let $G^{\prime}=G\cup F$ and assume that the described forbidden minor $[G^{\prime}]$ exists.
Since $G$ is $3$ -connected, it contains at least three internally vertex-disjoint paths $P_{a}$ , $P_{b}$ , and $P_{c}$ between $w$ and $x$ , where $a$ , $b$ , and $c$ are the first vertices along these paths not contained in $[w]^{-1}$ , respectively.
Furthermore, since $wx$ and $wy$ are retained in $[G^{\prime}]$ , $[x]^{-1}\cup[y]^{-1}$ does not contain $a$ , $b$ , or $c$ .
Also, since $[w]$ has degree two in $[G^{\prime}]\setminus[F]$ , we can choose these paths such that $[a]=[b]$ .
For any minor $[G^{\prime}]_{\star}$ in which $[w]_{\star}$ , $[x]_{\star}$ , and $[y]_{\star}$ are distinct vertices and $wx$ and $wy$ are retained, define $a([G^{\prime}]_{\star})$ to be the length of the longest path in $G^{\prime}$ that contains $a$ and consists only of vertices in $V(P_{a})\cap[a]_{\star}^{-1}$ .
Define $b([G^{\prime}]_{\star})$ similarly.
We will show how to obtain a minor $[G^{\prime}]_{1}$ from $[G^{\prime}]$ via a sequence of contractions and component exchanges such that (i) $[G^{\prime}]_{1}$ is a forbidden minor and $[w]_{1}$ , $[x]_{1}$ , and $[y]_{1}$ are distinct vertices and one of the following statements is true: (ii) either $wx$ or $wy$ is doubled in $[G^{\prime}]_{1}$ or (iii) $a([G^{\prime}]_{1})\geq a([G^{\prime}])$ , $b([G^{\prime}]_{1})\geq b([G^{\prime}])$ , and at least one of these inequalities is strict.
If Statements (i) and (ii) are true, then the lemma is clearly proved.
Otherwise, if Statements (i) and (iii) are true, then since $P_{a}$ and $P_{b}$ are each finite and end with $x$ , we can repeat this argument for $[G^{\prime}]_{1}$ , and so on, until we obtain a forbidden minor of $G^{\prime}$ for which Statements (i) and (ii) are true.
We complete the proof by showing how to obtain $[G^{\prime}]_{1}$ .
Let $a^{\prime}$ be the first vertex after $a$ in $P_{a}$ that is not contained in $[a]^{-1}$ .
Define $b^{\prime}$ similarly.
First, we show that we can wlog assume neither $a^{\prime}$ nor $b^{\prime}$ is contained in $[w]^{-1}$ .
If this is not the case, then assume wlog that $a^{\prime}\in[w]^{-1}$ .
Let $a^{\prime\prime}$ be the first vertex in $P_{a}$ after $a^{\prime}$ that is not contained in $[w]^{-1}$ , and define $S_{a^{\prime}}$ to be the path in $P_{a}$ between $a^{\prime}$ and the vertex that immediately precedes $a^{\prime\prime}$ .
Let $J$ be the subgraph of $G^{\prime}$ induced by $[w]^{-1}$ and let $J_{w}$ be the connected component of $J\setminus S_{a^{\prime}}$ that contains $w$ .
Since $P_{a}$ , $P_{b}$ , and $P_{c}$ are pairwise vertex-disjoint, $J_{w}$ contains every vertex along $P_{a}$ , $P_{b}$ , and $P_{c}$ between $w$ and the vertices immediately preceding $a$ , $b$ , and $c$ , respectively.
If some vertex in $J\setminus J_{w}$ neighbors some vertex in $[d]^{-1}$ , where $[d]$ is the neighbor of $[w]$ other than $[a]$ , $[x]$ , or $[y]$ , then let $[G^{\prime}]_{2}$ be the minor obtained via the component exchange from $[w]^{-1}$ to $[d]^{-1}$ that exchanges $J\setminus J_{w}$ .
Otherwise, let $[G^{\prime}]_{2}$ be the minor obtained via the component exchange from $[w]^{-1}$ to $[a]^{-1}$ that exchanges $J\setminus J_{w}$ .
Clearly, $[G^{\prime}]_{2}$ satisfies (i), $a([G^{\prime}]_{2})\geq a([G^{\prime}])$ , and $b([G^{\prime}]_{2})\geq b([G^{\prime}])$ .
Repeating this argument for $[G^{\prime}]_{2}$ , and so on, yields a minor with the desired property, which we can choose to be $[G^{\prime}]$ to begin with.
Next, let $H$ be the subgraph of $G^{\prime}$ induced by $[a]^{-1}$ .
Also, let $R_{a}$ be the path in $P_{a}$ between $a$ and the vertex that immediately precedes $a^{\prime}$ .
Define $R_{b}$ similarly.
Consider the connected component $H_{b}$ of $H\setminus R_{a}$ that contains $b$ , and let $H_{a}=H\setminus H_{b}$ .
Note that $H_{a}$ contains $R_{a}$ and $H_{b}$ contains $R_{b}$ .
Let $V_{a}$ be the subset of all vertices of $G^{\prime}\setminus([a]^{-1}\cup[w]^{-1})$ that the vertices in $H_{a}$ neighbor.
Define $V_{b}$ similarly.
Note that $a^{\prime}\in V_{a}$ and $b^{\prime}\in V_{b}$ since neither $a^{\prime}$ nor $b^{\prime}$ is contained in $[w]^{-1}$ .
There are two cases.
Case 1: $[G^{\prime}]$ is $K_{5}$ .
If $V_{a}$ is a subset of either $[x]^{-1}$ or $[y]^{-1}$ , then the minor $[G^{\prime}]_{1}$ obtained via the component exchange from $[a]^{-1}$ to $[V_{a}]^{-1}$ that exchanges $H_{a}$ satisfies Statements (i) and (ii).
Else, if $V_{a}$ is a subset of $[d]^{-1}$ , then the minor $[G^{\prime}]_{1}$ obtained via the component exchange from $[a]^{-1}$ to $[d]^{-1}$ that exchanges $H_{a}$ satisfies Statements (i) and (iii).
Otherwise, if $V_{a}$ is a subset of $[d]^{-1}\cup[x]^{-1}$ but not a subset $[d]^{-1}$ or $[x]^{-1}$ , then the minor $[G^{\prime}]_{1}$ obtained via the component exchange from $[a]^{-1}$ to $[y]^{-1}$ that exchanges $H_{b}$ satisfies Statements (i) and (ii).
A similar argument works if $V_{a}$ is a subset of $[d]^{-1}\cup[y]^{-1}$ but not a subset $[d]^{-1}$ or $[y]^{-1}$ .
If $V_{a}$ is a subset of $[x]^{-1}\cup[y]^{-1}$ but not a subset $[x]^{-1}$ or $[y]^{-1}$ , then $V_{b}$ contains some vertex in $[d]^{-1}$ .
Furthermore, using arguments similar to those above, we can assume $V_{b}$ contains some vertex in $[x]^{-1}\cup[y]^{-1}$ .
Wlog, if this vertex is contained in $[y]^{-1}$ , then the minor $[G^{\prime}]_{1}$ obtained via the component exchange from $[a]^{-1}$ to $[x]^{-1}$ that exchanges $H_{a}$ satisfies Statements (i) and (ii).
Lastly, the above arguments allow us to assume that $V_{a}$ and $V_{b}$ each contain vertices in $[d]^{-1}$ , $[x]^{-1}$ , and $[y]^{-1}$ , in which case the minor $[G^{\prime}]_{1}$ obtained via the component exchange from $[a]^{-1}$ to $[x]^{-1}$ that exchanges $H_{a}$ satisfies Statements (i) and (ii).
Case 2: $[G^{\prime}]$ is $K_{2,2,2}$ .
Wlog, assume that the neighborhood of $[a]$ is $\{[d],[e],[w],[x]\}$ , where $[e]$ is the vertex in $V([G^{\prime}])\setminus\{[a],[d],[w],[x],[y]\}$ .
If $V_{a}$ is a subset of $[x]^{-1}$ , then the minor $[G^{\prime}]_{1}$ obtained via the component exchange from $[a]^{-1}$ to $[x]^{-1}$ that exchanges $H_{a}$ satisfies Statements (i) and (ii).
Else, if $V_{a}$ is a subset of $[d]^{-1}$ , then the minor $[G^{\prime}]_{1}$ obtained via the component exchange from $[a]^{-1}$ to $[d]^{-1}$ that exchanges $H_{a}$ satisfies Statements (i) and (iii).
Otherwise, if $V_{a}$ is a subset of $[e]^{-1}$ , then consider the minor $[G^{\prime}]_{2}$ obtained via the component exchange from $[a]^{-1}$ to $[e]^{-1}$ that exchanges $H_{a}$ .
Observe that contracting $[bd]_{2}$ yields a $K_{5}$ minor $[G^{\prime}]_{3}$ in which $[w]_{3}$ , $[x]_{3}$ , and $[y]_{3}$ are distinct vertices and $wx$ and $wy$ are retained.
Hence, the existence of $[G^{\prime}]_{1}$ follows from Case 1.
Similar arguments apply for all remaining cases of $V_{a}$ .
The above cases are exhaustive, and so the lemma is proved.
∎
Lemma 38 (If $G\cup\{wx,wy,wz\}$ has a forbidden minor, then so does $G\cup\{wx,wy\}$ ).
Let $G$ be a $3$ -connected graph and $F=\{wx,wy,wz\}$ be some subset of its nonedges.
If there exists a forbidden minor $[G\cup F]$ in which $[w]$ , $[x]$ , $[y]$ , and $[z]$ are distinct vertices and $wx$ , $wy$ , and $wz$ are retained, then some size two subset $F^{\prime}$ of $F$ is such that $G\cup F^{\prime}$ has a forbidden minor.
Proof.
The proof is similar to the proof of Lemma 37.
Let $G^{\prime}=G\cup F$ and assume that $[G^{\prime}]$ exists.
Since $G$ is $3$ -connected, it contains at least two internally vertex-disjoint paths $P_{a}$ and $P_{b}$ between $w$ and $x$ , where $a$ and $b$ are the first vertices along these paths that are not contained in $[w]^{-1}$ , respectively.
Since $wx$ , $wy$ , and $wz$ are retained in $[G^{\prime}]$ , $[x]^{-1}\cup[y]^{-1}\cup[z]^{-1}$ does not contain $a$ or $b$ .
Also, since $[w]$ has degree one in $[G^{\prime}]\setminus[F]$ , we have $[a]=[b]$ .
We will show how to obtain a minor $[G^{\prime}]_{1}$ from $[G^{\prime}]$ via a sequence of contractions and component exchanges such that $[G^{\prime}]_{1}$ is a forbidden minor in which $[w]_{1}$ , $[x]_{1}$ , $[y]_{1}$ , and $[z]_{1}$ are distinct vertices and either $wx$ , $wy$ , or $wz$ is doubled.
If such a minor exists, then the lemma is clearly proved.
We complete the proof by showing how to obtain $[G^{\prime}]_{1}$ .
Let $a^{\prime}$ be the first vertex after $a$ in $P_{a}$ that is not contained in $[a]^{-1}$ .
Define $b^{\prime}$ similarly.
First, we show that we can wlog assume neither $a^{\prime}$ nor $b^{\prime}$ is contained in $[w]^{-1}$ .
If this is not the case, then assume that $a^{\prime}\in[w]^{-1}$ .
Since $[w]$ has degree one in $[G^{\prime}]\setminus[F]$ , the first vertex $a^{\prime\prime}$ after $a^{\prime}$ in $P_{a}$ that is not contained in $[w]^{-1}$ is contained in $[a]^{-1}$ .
Define $S_{a^{\prime}}$ to be the path in $P_{a}$ between $a^{\prime}$ and the vertex that immediately precedes $a^{\prime\prime}$ .
Let $J$ be the subgraph of $G^{\prime}$ induced by $[w]^{-1}$ and let $J_{w}$ be the connected component of $J\setminus S_{a^{\prime}}$ that contains $w$ .
Since $P_{a}$ and $P_{b}$ are vertex-disjoint, $J_{w}$ contains every vertex along $P_{b}$ between $w$ and the vertex immediately preceding $b$ .
Hence, the minor $[G^{\prime}]_{2}$ obtained via the component exchange from $[w]^{-1}$ to $[a]^{-1}$ that exchanges $J\setminus J_{w}$ is a forbidden minor in which $[w]_{2}$ , $[x]_{2}$ , $[y]_{2}$ , and $[z]_{2}$ are distinct vertices and $wx$ , $wy$ , and $wz$ are retained.
By repeating this argument for $[G^{\prime}]_{2}$ , and so on, we eventually obtain a minor with the desired property, which we can choose $[G^{\prime}]$ to be to begin with.
Next, let $H$ be the subgraph of $G^{\prime}$ induced by $[a]^{-1}$ .
Also, let $R_{a}$ be the path in $P_{a}$ between $a$ and the vertex that immediately precedes $a^{\prime}$ .
Define $R_{b}$ similarly.
Consider the connected component $H_{b}$ of $H\setminus R_{a}$ that contains $b$ , and let $H_{a}=H\setminus H_{b}$ .
Note that $H_{a}$ contains $R_{a}$ and $H_{b}$ contains $R_{b}$ .
Let $V_{a}$ be the subset of all vertices of $G\setminus([a]^{-1}\cup[w]^{-1})$ that the vertices in $H_{a}$ neighbor.
Define $V_{b}$ similarly.
Note that $a^{\prime}\in V_{a}$ and $b^{\prime}\in V_{b}$ since neither $a^{\prime}$ nor $b^{\prime}$ is contained in $[w]^{-1}$ .
There are two cases.
Case 1: $[G^{\prime}]$ is $K_{5}$ .
If $V_{a}$ is a subset of either $[x]^{-1}$ , $[y]^{-1}$ , or $[z]^{-1}$ , then the minor $[G^{\prime}]_{1}$ obtained via the component exchange from $[a]^{-1}$ to $[V_{a}]^{-1}$ that exchanges $H_{a}$ has the desired properties.
Else, if $V_{a}$ is a subset of the union of any two of these sets, wlog say $[x]^{-1}\cup[y]^{-1}$ , but not a subset of any one of these sets, then the minor $[G^{\prime}]_{1}$ obtained via the component exchange from $[a]^{-1}$ to $[z]^{-1}$ that exchanges $H_{b}$ has the desired properties.
Otherwise, if $V_{a}$ and $V_{b}$ each contain vertices in $[x]^{-1}$ , $[y]^{-1}$ , and $[z]^{-1}$ , then the minor $[G^{\prime}]_{1}$ obtained via the component exchange from $[a]^{-1}$ to $[x]^{-1}$ that exchanges $H_{a}$ has the desired properties.
Case 2: $[G^{\prime}]$ is $K_{2,2,2}$ .
Wlog, assume that the neighborhood of $[a]$ is $\{[d],[w],[x],[y]\}$ , where $[d]$ is the vertex in $V([G^{\prime}])\setminus\{[a],[w],[x],[y],[z]\}$ .
If $V_{a}$ is a subset of either $[x]^{-1}$ or $[y]^{-1}$ , then the minor obtained via the component exchange from $[a]^{-1}$ to $[V_{a}]^{-1}$ that exchanges $H_{a}$ has the desired properties.
Else, if $V_{a}$ is a subset of $[d]^{-1}$ , then consider the minor $[G^{\prime}]_{2}$ obtained via the component exchange from $[a]^{-1}$ to $[d]^{-1}$ that exchanges $H_{a}$ .
Contracting $[bx]_{2}$ yields a minor with the desired properties.
Otherwise, if $V_{a}$ is a subset of $[x]^{-1}\cup[y]^{-1}$ but not a subset of $[x]^{-1}$ or $[y]^{-1}$ , then the minor $[G^{\prime}]_{2}$ obtained via the component exchange from $[a]^{-1}$ to $[d]^{-1}$ that exchanges $H_{b}$ has the desired properties.
Similar arguments apply for all remaining cases of $V_{a}$ .
The above cases are exhaustive, and so the lemma is proved.
∎
Proof of Lemma 15.
The lemma follows immediately from Lemmas 37 and 38.
